<!--
Today, our immediate agenda to explore some advanced features of the TypeScript type system together.
First, we’ll establish a shared understanding and terminology for TypeScript’s type system through which we can communicate. We’ll the introduce a set of logical type system constructs that let us play around in the type system as if it was a traditional language. We’ll keep going with expanding those type system constructs until our brains too exploded to continue. It’s going to be a ton of fun.
-->

<!--
Now, while our agenda might be to implement silly type system games, our purpose is to gain a better understanding of how the TypeScript type system works, how to use it to improve our development practices, and what to do with all this newfound information.
For each concept introduced in this talk, we’ll start off with a real world example that calls for usage of that concept, then expand our type system game engine using that concept.
-->

</div>

<!--
All right!
Before we explore TypeScript’s type system, we should first establish a shared understanding and context of what that type system actually is. If you’re not yet familiar, I hope this will get you onboarded onto the basic premise of how it operates – don’t worry about absorbing the specific syntax forms.
If you’re already familiar with the type system, treat this as establishing the language we’ll use to describe operations in it.
-->

<!--
At its core, a dynamic type system is a reflection of your source code. It roughly understands the objects and logical flows you’ve set up in your source files and generates a rudimentary understanding of it. This type system is created when the TypeScript compiler runs on your source files, which is most often as part of a build process to generate JavaScript files, or as part of your IDE to give you useful features for development.
If you’ve ever seen Stranger Things or played Dungeons and Dragons, think of it as the Upside Down. It’s a dark husk of the real world, and although it is informed by the real world, barring some otherworldy meta-actions, it shouldn’t affect the behavior of the real world.
If you’ve experienced neither of those things, Instagram is an acceptable parallel as well! It’s a reflective shell of reality upon which we may judge ourselves. Scary.
“Upside Down” - https://www.bbc.com/news/newsbeat-44544409
“Instagram Post” - https://thepreviewapp.com/hide-post-instagram-feed/
-->

<!--
Let’s take a look at how TypeScript, JavaScript, the runtime, and the type system all play together.
A core promise of TypeScript is that your TypeScript source code will be transpiled into JavaScript, with any TypeScript-specific features such as type annotations erased. This is the mapping from TypeScript land to JavaScript land. When your code contains TypeScript constructs such as the ‘: string’ type annotation alongside JavaScript constructs such as this ‘trimUp’  arrow function, only the JavaScript constructs remain.
-->

transition: none

<!--
As part of the process of type checking your code, TypeScript generates that type system reflection of your raw source. In a sense this is the opposite of the JavaScript generation process: instead of pulling out just the runtime code, we pull out the type system reflections.
For our example so far, the type system will understand that we’ve declared a variable named ‘trimUp’, with a value equal to a function that takes in a single parameter of type string or undefined and returns a value of type string or undefined.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

</div>

<!--
Our first type system exploration will be “conditional generics”, which are a technique for generating types based on the properties of other types. It’s going to be the mechanism by which we introduce logic into the type system.
-->

---

---

<!--
Suppose you call the trimmedLength function from before on a string literal. TypeScript’s type system understanding of the function is that it always returns either a string or a number… but as human beings looking at this code, we can tell that trimmedLength returns a number (because its parameter is a string, not undefined).
TypeScript needs a way of understanding that if trimmedLength receives something we know is definitely a string, not undefined, it returns a number.
-->

transition: none
---

<div v-click="6" class="pptx-group"><div class="pptx-group" data-shape="21"><div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:29.08px;left:142.04px;top:451.24px;width:480px;justify-content:flex-end;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px;line-height:21.6px"><span style="color:#6A9955;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">// "</span><span style="color:#6A9955;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">Shut up TypeScript, I know what I’m doing!"</span></p></div><svg class="pptx-shape" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="339.51 288.9 184.41 219.05" style="height:219.05px;left:339.51px;top:288.9px;width:184.41px"><g transform="translate(142,352) translate(-146,-345)"><line x1="407" y1="436" x2="463" y2="345" stroke="rgb(0,0,0)" stroke-width="14" marker-end="url(#ah-s14-4001-t)" /></g></svg></div></div>
<div v-click="1" class="pptx-group"><div v-click.hide="5" class="pptx-group"><div class="pptx-shape" style="height:94.51px;left:129.29px;top:355.92px;width:701.42px;background-color:rgba(7,14,21,0.34);opacity:0.34"></div></div></div>
</div>

<!--
One common syntax to do so is shown here in declaring own ‘NumberIfDefined’ type in our TypeScript code. Let’s go through it line-by-line to really understand what’s happening.
NumberIfDefined is a type that takes in an existing type, known as a ‘generic’ or ‘template’, and creates a new type based on it.
The ‘extends’ clause makes it a conditional type: it tells the type system that the new type will be one of two possible types, based on whether the left hand side (T) could possibly be the right hand side (undefined).
If T can be undefined, which in this case is true for both ‘undefined’ and ‘string | undefined’, then the new type is ‘number | undefined’.
If T cannot be undefined, which in this case is true only for ‘string’, then the new type is only ‘number’.
Applying this idea to our function, we can provide a generic type to trimmedLength to indicate that the input parameter generally extends string | undefined, but could be more specific in another use case. This’ll be useful if we pass a value the type system knows to be just a string or just undefined, so TypeScript can pass that more specific type to our NumberIfDefined generic.
I’ll note that the ‘extends’ keyword used to declare the T type in trimmedLength is only coincidentally the same word as the ‘extends’ in the conditional type. The NumberIfDefined uses the word to establish a logical branch; trimmedLength uses the word to restrict the type of its ‘text’ parameter.
Lastly, we use an ‘as’ cast to tell TypeScript that the result of our code is something more fancy and precise than the ‘number | undefined’ it originally inferred. ‘as’ casting is our way of telling TypeScript to shut it; we know better what the code’s type should be.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

-->

<!--
Now the fun begin begins. Let’s take our conditional generic types and make a rudimentary Rock Paper Scissors matchup simulator. If you’ve never played Rock Paper Scissors before, the gist is that two people must simultaneously pick one of those three items. Each item wins against one other item, loses against another item, and draws against itself. Protip, it’s is a great way to pass time with small children.
This snippet encodes RockMatchup as a description of how a player’s Rock choice works against its possible opponents.
If the opponent is also ‘Rock’, it’s a draw.
If the opponent is ‘Paper’, the player’s Rock loses.
If the opponent is anything else (so, Scissors), the player’s Rock wins.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none

<!--
Fun fact: types may receive more than one generic. Here, we’ve defined the matchups for two players, with a bunch of nested checks for each of the nine possible results.
In the middle example RockVsPaper, Player is ‘Rock’ and Opponent is ‘Paper’, which resolves to a ‘Loss’ per the third ‘extends’ clause.
-->

<!--
Take a look at this simplified version of Lodash’s ‘get’ function. It takes in a container and a key, then returns the member of container under that key. How would you properly type this in TypeScript?
To start, let’s assume key is generally going to be either a string or a number… but that’s not particularly type safe. Ideally we’d want to restrict it to the known members of the container, not just any possible string or number.
We can use the generics to establish a specific per-function-call type T on container, but we’ll need a way to limit key to be one of the keys of that type.
Lo and behold, the ‘keyof’ operator in the type system exists for just this purpose. It takes in the type we need the keys of and spits back a union of just those keys.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none

<!--
We can now describe our Rock Paper Scissors matchups more succinctly by mapping from a Matchups type and checking our input Player and Opponent types against their members.
Our new and enhanced RockPaperScissors type takes in a Player and an Opponent, both of which must be one of the keys of Matchups: i.e. ‘Rock’, ‘Paper’, or ‘Scissors’.
If the Opponent type is one of the members of the Matchup’s listing under the Player, we go with that value.
Otherwise, we assume it’s a Draw.
-->

transition: none

style="color:#4EC9B0;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">Error</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px"> | </span><span style="color:#4EC9B0;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">T</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">)[]): [</span><span style="color:#4EC9B0;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">Error</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">[], </span><span style="color:#4EC9B0;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">T</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">[]] </span><span style="color:#569CD6;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">=&gt;</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px"> &#123;</span></p><p style="font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px;line-height:21.6px"><span style="color:#569CD6;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">    const </span><span style="color:#4FC1FF;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">errors</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">: </span><span style="color:#4EC9B0;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">Error</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">[] = [];</span></p><p style="font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px;line-height:21.6px"><span style="color:#569CD6;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">    const </span><span style="color:#4FC1FF;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">successes</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">: </span><span style="color:#4EC9B0;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">T</span><span style="color:#D4D4D4;font-family:'Consolas';font-size:18px">[] = [];</span></p></div></div>
</div>

<!--
Take this splitErrors function, which takes in an array of results and splits them into two other arrays based on being an Error or not an Error.
Per the style recently popularized by React hooks, these are returned as a fixed size array, or “tuple”, where:
The first member is known to be an errors array.
The second member is known to be the non-error results.
First off, we know this is going to be a generic function where the results are an array of items such that each item is either an error or the T templated type. The ‘errors’ variable is an array of Errors; the ‘successes’ variable is an array of successes.
Second, although TypeScript infers the returned array to also be containing members that are each either Error or T, we’d like to indicate it’s a fixed size “tuple” with specific members. Adding an explicit return type annotation to the splitErrors function will specify the exact details on that array.
This brings us to the topic of tuple types, referring to these arrays of fixed sizes and well-known members. And you know what other use case is satisfied by tuples?
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

<!--
Board game boards!
We can declare a TicTacToeBoard type as a tuple of tuples, representing a grid of spots on a 3x3 board. Each spot may be filled with a blank character, an X, or an O.
-->

<!--
Such a board, by the way, is the nature of Tic Tac Toe. Players alternate putting down pieces until someone gets three pieces in a row or the board is filled.
Thus, we can create a conditional Victory type in the type system that takes in a board and returns a new type representing whether that board any of the three possible victory conditions for that board: three-in-a-row diagonally, horizontally, or vertically.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

<!--
The diagonal win condition for a player is hit when either of the diagonal lines on the board can only be that player’s pieces. The Cell spots can be filled with anything, but the Player spots must be filled with only the player’s pieces.
Similarly, the horizontal victory check is satisfied only when one of the three rows in the board are known to contain only the player’s pieces. Same with the vertical victory check and the board’s columns.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

<!--
Making use of the WinningBoard type, we can see that a WinAtStart check for a board comprised entirely of blanks is false. None of the 8 win conditions are satisfied by that board.
If we add a few pieces and include a diagonal victory for the checked piece, however, our result becomes true. Success!
-->

<!--
Taking our conditionals one step higher, we can check which player –if either– wins by seeing whether the board satisfies the victory condition for either of our known players. Nifty.
The blank starting board doesn’t satisfy the win conditions for either player, so it returns the blank piece. Our second game board satisfies the Winner type’s second conditional, for a ‘O’ victory, so it returns a ‘O’.
-->

---

layout: none

<!--
So far we’ve gone over logical types, type members, and arrays of types. We’ve gained the ability to make targeted decisions in our types on single cases.
In this section, we’ll introduce ways to transform all members of types in our type decision making, which will open the doors to much more grandiose dynamic type system type generation.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

<!--
Before we dive too far into the type system, let’s hop back into runtime land and look at this simplified version of Lodash’ “pick” function. It takes in an original “container” and an array of keys, then returns a new object containing just those keys from the container. How would you type it?
Referring back to our previous Lodash “get” experimentation, we know how to type these function parameters: using a templated type and a keyof on that type.
That doesn’t take care of the output type, though. What should we say ‘picked’ is?
-->

transition: none
---

<!--
One way to represent the resultant ‘picked’ object is with a mapped type.  Mapped types take in a list of keys, such as from a keyof operator, and create new type members based on them.
This ‘Picked’ mapped type takes in two template types, a Container and a union of some of its keys. For each key in those keys, the resultant type’s member under that key is the same as the original container’s.
In other words, we’ve picked a selection of members from the original Container type and copied them into our new type.
-->

transition: none

<!--
Now, using our new Picked type, we can cast the picked variable to be a pick of the allowed keys on the container. We’ve now used mapped types to somewhat more properly type Lodash’s pick.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

<div class="pptx" style="background-color:rgb(7,14,21)">

<!--
As a side note, the Picked type we just implemented is already available in TypeScript as ‘Pick’, along a dozen other useful built-in type system utilities.
The Partial type, which adds the ? (optional) modifier to all members, is one of my favorites. It’s great for setting up default arguments in unit tests for functions or React components.
The Readonly type, which adds the readonly modifier to all members, is great too. It’s useful for functional-style codebases where you don’t want to let code modify certain objects.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

</div>

-->

<!--
We can use our new mapped types to describe a Tic Tac Toe board in a bit more dynamic of a manner.
Under this description, any object that has arrays under 0, 1, and 2 which themselves contain Cells under 0, 1, and 2 match the board. That’ll come in handy later when we set up mapped types so weird that TypeScript forgets the result was supposed to be an array.
-->

<!--
Now that we know how to map all members of a type into a new type, we should be able to apply that technique to placing pieces on a Tic Tac Toe board. Given our starting board from before and a desire to place ‘X’ at row 0 / column 1, we should be able to use conditional mapped types to take in our board tuples and output a modified board.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

<div class="pptx" style="background-color:rgb(7,14,21)">

…and just like that, we’ve gained the ability to place pieces on a board.
I’ll also note here that although our ReplaceInBoard type works, it’s pushing the boundaries of what the type system is really intended for, so it loses just a little bit of clarity. TypeScript sees the resultant type as an object with number keys.
-->

<!--
Suppose you want to make a type that refers to the first parameter of another type, which happens to be a function. How would you do that?
Clarifying: if the other type takes in a single argument of type string, your resultant type would be a string.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none

</div>

<!--
The answer is an inferred type. Introduced alongside conditional types back in TypeScript 2.8, these allow you to extract an inferred type from a larger type in a conditional. This is useful when you know you want to take something from an existing type, say, the first parameter of a function.
In ‘FirstParamOf’, we set up a generic type, then check whether it extends from a function that has a single parameter – storing a reference to that inferred parameter as FirstParam. If that extends is valid, we return FirstParam. If not, we return ‘never’, the type indicating nothing is there (also known as a “bottom” type).
-->

layout: none
transition: none
---

<!--
Extracting inferred types from functions is itself also provided to us by a couple more of those built-in functions we saw earlier. Thanks, TypeScript!
ReturnType infers the R return type from a function in a similar manner to our FirstParamOf.
Parameters does as well, by inferring the array type of the spread arguments of the function. (If arguments spreading is a meaningless term to you, fun fact, this is the syntax to let P refer to an array of arguments passed to the inferred function parameters.)
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

<!--
Back to the game boards, what I’d like to do now is take a starting board and an array of moves, then generate how the board should look after all of them have been applied. This’ll let us simulate a full-on game of Tic Tac Toe based on the move inputs.
This is difficult because there’s no “for loop” operator in the type system. We can’t set up a type and imperatively modify it the way we would in, say, JavaScript.
TypeScript’s type system is closer to a functional language (or even a logical one), which is to say nothing can be modified after creation, and all inferences must be made based on already defined truths.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

<div class="pptx" style="background-color:rgb(7,14,21)">
<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:104.38px;left:66px;top:28.75px;width:828px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><p style="padding-left:-0.2px;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:44px;line-height:47.52px;text-align:center"><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:44px">Thinking Functionally</span></p></div>

<!--
If we were working in runtime code, we’d tackle this functional problem recursively. There are two possibilities when calling our applyMoves function:
If the remaining moves are empty, we can return the board as-is
If there’s at least one move to apply, we apply it, then recurse on the new board with the remaining moves
-->

<!--
Our code in the type system looks a little different but amounts to the same result.
Instead of an applyMoves function with a lower-case a, we have a generic ApplyMoves type with a capital A. It still takes in a board and array of moves.
First, if the moves extends the empty array (so, if there are no more moves), the resultant type is the board itself.
Otherwise, we return a resultant type from ApplyMoves. That recursive type system call to ApplyMoves takes in the result of applying our first move to the board for a board, and all but the first move from our moves as its array of moves.
The remaining moves are calculated using a DropFirst type that shows off why we just learned about inferred types. DropFirst takes in a T that must be an array, then checks if it’s possible for a remaining array (using the …, or spread, operator) to be inferred from all but the first list member. If that array is possible, it’s the resultant type. If not, an empty array is returned.
By the way, if you’re following along at home, this might not work on your version of TypeScript. Recursive types like our ApplyMoves are only going to be permitted in version 4.1 and higher of TypeScript. Get excited for new TypeScript versions!
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none

<!--
If we run our ApplyMoves type on our blank StartingBoard and an array type containing a couple of moves, we get back an output type with those two moves applied.
-->

<!--
Amazing! Yes! Hooray! By harnessing the power of inferred types and joining them with recursive type definitions, we’re able to apply a list of moves stored in the type system to a board. Our output board is the result of all those moves applied on top of each other.
“Spongebob Squarepants Reaction GIF By Feliks Tomasz Konczakowski” - https://giphy.com/gifs/spongebob-squarepants-endless-cartoon-3o7aCZDlmQZLe4Q4V2
-->

The last major type system feature I’d like to show off today is also a new one, and is in my opinion one of the stranger –though still very useful– additions to TypeScript. It’s also only available on versions 4.1 later.
-->

<!--
Suppose you want to write a function that takes in an arbitrary template string, a key to swap in the template, and a value for that key. The template string contains the key wrapped in two curly brackets as an indication of where to place the new value.
How would you do it?
At a basic level, we know all three parameters are technically strings… but wouldn’t it be nice to have some type safety that the provided key exists in our template string?
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

<!--
Thanks to the pioneering efforts of Anders and the TypeScript team, the next version of TypeScript will have template literal string types, which allow us to check for and extract substrings from inferred string type in the type system.
Here, I’ve set up a KeyForTemplate generic type that takes in a Template string and checks if it extends a type system template literal. That template literal contains five parts:
An inferred “Prefix” type, which isn’t used anywhere else
Two left curly brackets
An inferred “Key” type, which is the returned type of in the true case of the conditional
Two right curly brackets
AN inferred “Suffix” type, which isn’t used anywhere else
In other words, if the Template type matches a string containing any prefix, two left curly brackets, any middle portion, two right curly brackets, and any suffix, we return the middle portion of the format. If not, the type is invalid.
You can see the results in the code snippets below. Our first ‘interpolate’ call gives no type system errors because the template string does match that format. Its ignored Prefix is “Hi, “; its middle Key is “name”, and its ignored Suffix is an exclamation mark.
If our template doesn’t match the suggested format, such as with our second interpolate call’s “{{NOPE}}”, the key type resolves to ‘never’, and we get an unhappy type system complaint for the call.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:342.63px;left:586.43px;top:143.75px;width:373.57px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><ol><li style="font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px;line-height:30.24px;margin-top:10px;color:#C8D7FA;list-style-type:decimal;margin-left:40.5px"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px">Convert the raw moves description into a list of moves</span></li><li style="font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px;line-height:30.24px;margin-top:10px;color:#C8D7FA;list-style-type:decimal;margin-left:40.5px"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px">Pass that list of moves to our already-written </span><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px">ApplyMoves</span><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px"> type</span></li><li style="font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px;line-height:30.24px;margin-top:10px;color:#C8D7FA;list-style-type:decimal;margin-left:40.5px"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px">Pass that result to our already-written </span><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px">Winner</span><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px"> type</span></li></ol></div>
</div>

<!--
Keeping the type system’s functional/logical nature in mind, here’s my proposal for how we’re going to tackle this TicTacToe type.
First, we’ll convert the raw moves description string into a list of move descriptions using a combination of the string template literal types we just saw and the recursive array building technique from applying move tuples.
Next, we’ll take the ApplyMoves type we already wrote out and pass the list of moves to it, to generate an end state board.
Finally, we’ll pass that end-state board to our already-written Winner type to get out the result of who wins.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

<!--
Since we already wrote out ApplyMoves and Victory in previous slides, I won’t show them here. Instead let’s start with the root TicTacToe type applying the algorithm we just described.
The innermost call of ParseRawMoves to take in the moves and return a list of moves is the first thing it does. That result is then passed to ApplyMoves alongside the starting board. Its result is then passed to Winner to figure out who won.
-->

<div class="pptx-shape pptx-text" style="height:342.63px;left:586.43px;top:122.42px;width:373.57px;justify-content:center;padding-bottom:3.6px;padding-left:7.2px;padding-right:7.2px;padding-top:3.6px"><ol><li style="font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px;line-height:30.24px;margin-top:10px;color:#C8D7FA;list-style-type:decimal;margin-left:40.5px"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px">Split the steps into an array on </span><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px">\n </span><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px">endlines</span></li><li style="font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px;line-height:30.24px;margin-top:10px;color:#C8D7FA;list-style-type:decimal;margin-left:40.5px"><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px">Filter those move lines into an array of usable </span><span style="color:#FFFFFF;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px">Move </span><span style="color:#C8D7FA;font-family:'Regular Bold';font-size:28px">tuples</span></li></ol></div>

<!--
Here’s the base algorithm for parsing those move strings. It’s got two types inside of it that we’ll have to implement.
First, we use a Split type to take in the moves and split on endline. That’ll be the first thing we have to implement.
Then, we take the result of that and pass it to a CollectParsedRawMoves type, which parses those raw moves and puts the into an array. This happens to pass an empty array and an ‘X’ string, which we’ll use in that type as the starting moves list and piece to place.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none

<!--
So, string splitting! We’re back to using template strings to parse out inferred substrings. This is actually a simplified form of a type mentioned in the pull request that added template literal types to TypeScript. Thanks, Anders!
This Split type takes in a Text string and a Splitter string. If the text is empty, then we’re done, it gives back an empty array.
Otherwise, it checks for an instance of the Splitter string inside the Text. If there is, the result is first whatever’s before the Splitter string, followed by a recursive call to split whatever’s after the Splitter. If the Splitter wasn’t found, then this string can no longer be split:  we return an array containing the all of the Text.
-->

---

layout: none
transition: none
---

<!--
Great. Now that we can split the string on endlines, we’ve got one last challenge ahead of us: turning these lines into raw move descriptions.
You may recall from before that our Move type is a tuple with three members:
A piece, as either an ‘X’ string or an ‘O’,
The row to place, as 0, 1, or 2,
The column to place, also as 0, 1, or 2
-->

transition: none
---

<!--
The full CollectParsedRawMoves type is a little hairy so I’ve split out a few pieces of logic into helpers shown here.
The NextTurn type takes in a Turn string and returns the other possible one. We’ll use this to switch between which turn we’re trying to parse out of a raw move.
The IntToString type takes in a string description of a row or column and gives back the integer equivalent. It’ll be used in the third helper, ParseRawMove, which takes in a turn, a string version of a row, and a string version of a column, then gives back the Move equivalent (with integers).
-->

<!--
Ok dokie! Here it is! CollectParsedRawMoves! A type that recursively goes through an array of raw move strings and turns them into our nice Move tuple types. Glorious.
It’s kind of big, so let’s go through it line by line.
Our CollectParsedRawMoves type takes in the array of raw moves, the collected moves thus far, and a current turn to parse out of the next raw move.
If there are no more raw moves to parse out, it’s done, hooray!, and returns an empty array.
If there is a next move to parse, it checks whether that move matches a usable pattern. There can be any amount of Pre text before the Turn string we’re trying to parse out, followed by a space, the row, another space, the column, then any amount of Post text. If it did match, then that’s great, we can create a new list of moves containing the previous list followed by this new move as parsed by our ParseRawMove helper. That new list of moves will be what we recurse on, along with all but the first raw move, and the next turn.
If our raw move doesn’t match the template, such as with that blank line we saw at the beginning of the raw moves array, then we still recurse with the rest of the raw moves, but keep collected the same and try again with the same turn.
-->

transition: none

<!--
If you have time to waste, I’d love to see what else you can do with this system. I’m sure there are little inconsistencies or cleanups you could make on the code as presented. I had to take a shortcut or two for the presentation, mentioned on the companion blog post.
Beyond that, you could definitely extend the use cases of this exploration. Maybe a win/loss calculator to take in an array of move descriptions and show the number of wins, losses, and draws? A game AI that chooses a next optimal move for a board?
Beyond tic tac toe, I’d love to see more games implemented. Connect Four is probably a good next step. Checkers and even chess might be fun challenges too, given that TypeScript limits recursion depths to 50 moves. Maybe a Boggle solver for very small Boggle boards and dictionaries?
-->

<!--
Much more usefully than anything before, I’d definitely recommend you check out DefinitelyTyped, the open source repository that stores TypeScript type definitions for packages that don’t ship with their own.
-->

<!--
It’s a huge repository with thousands of open issues, ready for you to tackle. If you use any JavaScript libraries that aren’t (yet?) written in TypeScript, applying the fun concepts you saw here today to flesh them out is a wonderful way to spend your time.
This month is even Hacktoberfest, a promotion wherein you get a free shirt for sending pull requests on GitHub. It’s perfect!
-->
